That[2K⋯ is a clear and[2K⋯ unmistakable delegation.  2. **[2K⋯ Is the delegation clause itself[2K⋯ enforce[2K⋯ able?** A[2K⋯ delegation clause is treated[2K⋯ like[2K⋯ any other contract term under[2K⋯ FAA § 2 (Pr[2K⋯ ima Paint; Buckeye Check[2K⋯ C[2K⋯ ashing; Rent-A-Center West[2K⋯ v[2K⋯ . Jackson).   ▐ aura  Done. I read `case-05.md` and answered its Question section in `ANSWER.md` (verified on disk).

The case is an arbitration delegation-clause dispute (the facts track *Sandquist v. Lebo Automotive* / *Rent-A-Center*). The answer, in brief:

- **The clause is a delegation clause** — "exclusive authority" over any dispute about the agreement's "interpretation, applicability, or enforceability," reinforced by incorporated provider rules, so it is a "clear and unmistakable" delegation under the FAA (*First Options*; *Rent-A-Center*).
- **Four issues presented**: (1) who decides arbitrability; (2) the Employee's specific unconscionability attack on the delegation clause itself; (3) whether class arbitration is available; (4) how broadly to construe and enforce the clause.
- **Construction/enforcement**: the clause is construed broadly and enforced as written — the court decides only the specific attack on the delegation clause (which fails: standard, symmetrical language; at most modest procedural unconscionability, offset; her "in combination" argument is really an attack on the agreement as a whole, which belongs to the arbitrator). The arbitrator then decides the agreement's overall unconscionability and the class-arbitration question; the class waiver itself is enforceable (*Concepcion*; *Epic Systems*). The writ should be denied.
- The file ends with the requested one-line justification.

One note: web verification was unavailable in this session (no search service connected, and en.wikipedia.org isn't in the project's scope list — you can allow it with `/scope add en.wikipedia.org` if you want cited sources checked online), so the answer rests on the case's own facts plus established doctrine.
